Computationally Efficient and Statistically Optimal Robust Low-rank Matrix and Tensor Estimation
Yinan Shen, Jingyang Li, Jian-Feng Cai, Dong Xia

Abstract
Low-rank matrix estimation under heavy-tailed noise is challenging, both computationally and statistically. Convex approaches have been proven statistically optimal but suffer from high computational costs, especially since robust loss functions are usually non-smooth. More recently, computationally fast non-convex approaches via sub-gradient descent are proposed, which, unfortunately, fail to deliver a statistically consistent estimator even under sub-Gaussian noise. In this paper, we introduce a novel Riemannian sub-gradient (RsGrad) algorithm which is not only computationally efficient with linear convergence but also is statistically optimal, be the noise Gaussian or heavy-tailed with a finite 1 + ε 1+\varepsilon moment. Convergence theory is established for a general framework and specific applications to absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss are investigated. Compared with existing non-convex methods, ours reveals a surprising phenomenon of dual-phase convergence . In phase one, RsGrad behaves as in a typical non-smooth optimization that requires gradually decaying stepsizes. However, phase one only delivers a statistically sub-optimal estimator which is already observed in existing literature. Interestingly, during phase two, RsGrad converges linearly as if minimizing a smooth and strongly convex objective function and thus a constant stepsize suffices. Underlying the phase-two convergence is the smoothing effect of random noise to the non-smooth robust losses in an area close but not too close to the truth. Lastly, RsGrad is applicable for low-rank tensor estimation under heavy-tailed noise where statistically optimal rate is attainable with the same phenomenon of dual-phase convergence, and a novel shrinkage-based second-order moment method is guaranteed to deliver a warm initialization. Numerical simulations confirm our theoretical discovery and showcase the superiority of RsGrad over prior methods.



1
Introduction

Let
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
be a
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix of rank
r\ll d_{2}
where, without loss of generality, we assume
d_{1}\geq d_{2}
. Denote
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
the collection of i.i.d. observations satisfying

Y_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{i},{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\rangle+\xi_{i}

(1)

with
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
known as the
measurement matrix
. Here,
\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{i},{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\rangle={\rm tr}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})
and hence (
1
) is often called the
trace regression
model. See, e.g.,
Koltchinskii 2011
;
Koltchinskii and Xia 2015
;
Rohde and Tsybakov 2011
and references therein. The latent noise
\xi_{i}
has mean zero and is usually assumed
random
for ease of analysis.

Noisy low-rank matrix estimation
or
matrix sensing
refers to the goal of recovering
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
from the observations
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. This problem and its variants naturally arise in diverse fields — quantum state tomography
(
Gross et al. 2010
;
Xia and Koltchinskii 2016
)
, multi-task learning
(
Chen et al. 2011
;
Negahban and Wainwright 2011
)
, structured model inference
(
Chiu et al. 2021
;
Siddiqi et al. 2010
)
, to name but a few. Oftentimes, the dimensions
d_{1},d_{2}
are large. It is of great interest to effectively, both computationally and statistically, recover
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
using as few measurements as possible.

The past two decades have witnessed a vast literature on designing computationally efficient methods and investigating their statistical performances and limits for estimating the underlying low-rank matrix. At the common ground of these methods is a carefully selected loss function that leverages the low-rank structure. Estimating
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
then boils down to solving an optimization program. Towards that end, all these methods take an either
convex
or
non-convex
approach. Typical convex approaches introduce an additional penalization by matrix nuclear norm into the loss function to promote low-rank solutions. This convexity renders well-understood algorithms for convex programming immediately applicable. Therefore statisticians can just focus on studying the statistical performances without paying too much attention to the computational implementations. For instance, by assuming i.i.d.
sub-Gaussian
noise with variance
\sigma^{2}
and the so-called
restricted isometry property
(RIP) or
restricted strong convexity
(RSC), it has been proved
(
Candes and Plan 2011
;
Rohde and Tsybakov 2011
;
Negahban and Wainwright 2011
;
Cai and Zhang 2015
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Davenport and Romberg 2016
;
Chandrasekaran et al. 2012
)
that the nuclear-norm penalized least square estimator, referred to as the convex-
\ell_{2}
approach, attains the error rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sigma^{2}rd_{1}n^{-1})
1
1

1

Here
\tilde{O}_{p}(\cdot)
stands for the typical big-O notation up to logarithmic factors and holds with high probability.
in squared Frobenius norm. This rate is shown to be optimal in the minimax sense. See, e.g.,
Xia 2014
;
Ma and Wu 2015
and references therein. Despite the appealing theoretical performances, convex approaches suffer two major drawbacks. First, convex methods operate directly on a matrix of size
d_{1}\times d_{2}
making them run slowly and unscalable to ultra-high dimensional problems. Secondly, while the established error rate is theoretically minimax optimal, the implicit constant factor seems large and the resultant error rate is,
in practice
, often inferior to that by non-convex approaches. Fortunately, non-convex approaches can nicely address these two drawbacks. The starting point of non-convex methods is to re-parametrize the optimization program by writing
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}={\mathbf{U}}^{\ast}{\mathbf{V}}^{\ast\top}
with
{\mathbf{U}}^{\ast}
and
{\mathbf{V}}^{\ast}
both having
r
columns, i.e., requiring the knowledge of rank. It then suffices to minimize the loss with respect to the factors
{\mathbf{U}}
and
{\mathbf{V}}
by, for instance, the projected gradient descent
(
Chen and Wainwright 2015
)
, Burer-Monteiro type gradient descent
(
Burer and Monteiro 2003
;
Zheng and Lafferty 2016
)
, rotation-calibrated gradient descent
(
Zhao et al. 2015
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
)
, Riemannian gradient descent
(
Wei et al. 2016
)
and etc. Compared to the convex counterparts, these non-convex algorithms operate on matrix of size
d_{1}\times r
directly and easily scales to large-scale setting even when
d_{1}
is about millions. Under similar conditions and with a non-trivial initialization, it has been demonstrated that these non-convex algorithms converge fast (more exactly, linearly) and also deliver minimax optimal estimators under sub-Gaussian noise.

The recent boom of data technology poses new challenges to noisy low-rank matrix estimation, among which heavy-tailed noise has appeared routinely in numerous applications such as diffusion-weighted imaging
(
Chang et al. 2005
)
, on-line advertising
(
Sun et al. 2017
)
, and gene-expression data analysis
(
Sun et al. 2020
)
. Actually, the aforementioned convex and non-convex approaches with a square loss become vulnerable or even completely useless when the noise has a heavy tail. It is because that heavy-tailed noise often generates a non-negligible set of outliers, to which the square loss is sensitive. To mitigate the effect of heavy-tailed noise, a natural solution is to replace the square loss by more robust but
non-smooth
ones. Notable examples include the
\ell_{1}
-loss
(
Candès et al. 2011
;
Cambier and Absil 2016
)
, the renowned Huber loss
(
Huber 1965
)
and quantile loss
(
Koenker and Hallock 2001
)
, all of which are convex but non-smooth. For instance,
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
proposes a convex approach based on nuclear-norm penalized
\ell_{1}
or Huber loss, and proves that their estimator, under a mild condition, attains the error rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(rd_{1}n^{-1})
in squared Frobenius norm as long as the noise has a non-zero density in a neighbourhood of origin. The rate is minimax optimal in terms of the model complexity. A more general framework requiring only Lipschitz and convex loss but imposing no assumption on the noise is investigated by
Alquier et al. 2019
, though the scenario of heavy-tailed noise is not specifically discussed. See also
Klopp et al. 2017
. The application of foregoing approaches still rely on convex programming and suffer from the computational issues explained above. In fact, the issue is severer here due to the non-smoothness of objective function
(
Boyd et al. 2004
)
. In consideration of computational efficiency, non-convex approaches have been proposed and investigated for minimizing non-smooth but convex loss functions. The sub-Gradient descent algorithms based on matrix factorization were studied recently by
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021b
, and all of them converge linearly if equipped with a good initialization and with properly chosen stepsizes. While being computationally fast, the statistical performances of these non-convex approaches under heavy-tailed noise are either largely missing or, generally, sub-optimal. To be more specific,
Li et al. 2020
only proves the exact recovery under sparse outliers
without noise
; the rate in squared Frobenius norm established in
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
and
Li et al. 2020
turns into
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sigma^{2})
even if the noise are Gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}
. This rate has none statistical significance since it implies that more data (i.e.,
n
increases) will not improve the estimate. Put it differently, these estimators are not even statistically consistent. Besides the aforementioned optimization-oriented approaches,
Minsker 2018
and
Fan et al. 2021
propose computationally fast estimators based on proper shrinkage and low-rank approximation. Though the rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(rd_{1}n^{-1})
is achievable, it is not proportional to the noise size, even for sub-Gaussian noise, leaving a considerable gap as noise level varies.
These prior works all present unpleasant defects in treating low-rank matrix estimation under heavy-tailed noise.

To bridge the aforementioned gap, in this paper, we propose a computationally efficient non-convex algorithm based on Riemannian sub-gradient (RsGrad) optimization and demonstrate its statistical optimality for several mainstream robust loss functions including absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss. At the core of RsGrad is to view the set of rank-
r
matrices as a smooth manifold so that established manifold-based optimization methods are readily applicable
(
Vandereycken 2013
;
Cambier and Absil 2016
)
, among which Riemannian (sub-)gradient descent is perhaps the most popular. See, e.g. ,
Wei et al. 2016
;
Cai et al. 2021a
and references therein. Similarly as
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021b
, with a proper scheduling of stepsizes, RsGrad algorithm admits fast computation and converges linearly. Surprisingly, through a more sophisticated analysis, we discover that RsGrad exhibits an intriguing, referred to as
dual-pahse convergence
, phenomenon. In phase one, RsGrad behaves like a typical
non-smooth
optimization
(
Tong et al. 2021b
)
, e.g., requiring gradually decaying stepsizes through iterations. For example, if the noise is Gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}
and an absolute loss is equipped, the phase-one iterations reach an estimator whose squared Frobenius norm is
O_{p}(\sigma^{2})
. This rate has been achieved by
Tong et al. 2021b
whereas theirs is based on a scaled sub-gradient (ScaledSM) descent algorithm. However, we reveal a phase two convergence of RsGrad. In phase two, while the objective function is still non-smooth (but locally smooth in a small region), the convergence of RsGrad behaves like a smooth optimization in that a constant stepsize guarantees a linear convergence. As a result, after phase-two iterations, RsGrad can output an estimator which is statistically optimal under mild conditions, e.g. it achieves the rate
O_{p}(\sigma^{2}rd_{1}n^{-1})
for the previous example. Similar results are also established for other robust loss functions under general heavy-tailed noise.

The merits of RsGrad are further demonstrated on robust low-rank (Tucker) tensor estimation. A tensor is a multi-dimensional array, e.g., a matrix is a
2
nd-order tensor. The trace regression model (
1
) is frequently studied for low-rank tensor estimation where
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
and all
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}
’s are tensors. By unfolding a tensor into matrices,
Tomioka and Suzuki 2013
;
Mu et al. 2014
proposed convex methods using matrix nuclear norms which, unfortunately, only achieve statistically sub-optimal rates under Gaussian noise and demand high computational costs. A delicate tensor nuclear norm was introduced by
Raskutti et al. 2019
which, albeit statistically appealing, is computationally NP-hard in general. In comparison, non-convex methods via direct tensor decomposition often simultaneously enjoy the statistical optimality and computational efficiency. Representative works in this line include the projected gradient descent algorithm
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
, penalized jointly gradient descent
(
Han et al. 2022
)
, Grassmannian gradient descent
(
Lyu et al. 2021
)
, Riemannian gradient descent
(
Cai et al. 2021a
)
and etc. Statistical performances of the aforementioned works are guaranteed under sub-Gaussian noise and considerably deteriorate when the noise have heavy tails. During the preparation of this work, we noticed that
Tong et al. 2022
extends the ScaledSM algorithm to robust low-rank tensor estimation that naturally handles heavy-tailed noise. However, as discussed above, the statistical rate achieved by ScaledSM even under Gaussian noise is sub-optimal and stays still even if the sample size
n
increases. Interestingly, our RsGrad algorithm equipped with robust loss functions is easily applicable to heavy-tailed low-rank tensor estimation. We establish a similar dual-phase convergence and derive statistically optimal rates under a minimal
1+\varepsilon
moment condition and a nearly optimal sample size requirement. This clearly fills the void in robust low-rank tensor estimation under heavy-tailed noise.

Our contributions are multi-fold. First, we propose a computationally efficient method for robust low-rank matrix estimation that is applicable to a wide class of non-smooth loss functions. While Riemannian sub-gradient descent (RsGrad) has been introduced by
Cambier and Absil 2016
for minimizing the absolute loss, to our best knowledge, there exists no theoretical guarantees for its convergence. Under mild conditions, we prove that RsGrad converges linearly regardless of the condition number of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
making it the preferable algorithm for estimating an ill-conditioned matrix. Secondly, we demonstrate the statistical optimality of the final estimator delivered by RsGrad, be the noise Gaussian or heavy-tailed. Specific applications to the absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss confirm that a rate
O_{p}(rd_{1}n^{-1})
is attainable as long as the noise has a fine
1+\varepsilon
moment and its density satisfies mild regularity conditions. The same conditions have appeared in
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
. Unlike
Fan et al. 2021
and
Minsker 2018
, our rate is proportional of the noise size. Thirdly, our analysis reveals a new phenomenon of
dual-phase convergence
that enables us to achieve statistically optimal error rates, which is a significant improvement over existing literature
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021b
)
. While the phase-one convergence is typical for a non-smooth optimization, the phase-two convergence, interestingly, behaves like a smooth optimization. It seems that the random noise has an effect of smoothing in the phase-two convergence. Though our dual-phase convergence is only established for RsGrad, we believe that this should also occur for the factor-based sub-gradient descent
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
)
and the scaled sub-gradient descent
(
Li et al. 2020
)
. Finally, we derive the statistically optimal rate of low-rank tensor estimation under heavy-tailed noise. Under a slightly stronger
2+\varepsilon
moment condition, we propose a novel shrinkage-based second-order moment method that guarantees a warm initialization under a nearly optimal sample size and signal-to-noise condition. To our best knowledge, these are the first results of this kind in tensor-related literature.

The rest of paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce examples of non-smooth robust loss functions and our Riemannian sub-gradient descent (RsGrad) algorithm in a general framework. Section
3
presents the general convergence theory of RsGrad, i.e., dual-phase convergence, under the dual-phase regularity condition of loss function. Specific applications to absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss are investigated for both Gaussian noise and heavy-tailed noise in Section
4
. Methods of initialization and discussions about stepsize selection are provided in Section
5
. Section
6
extends RsGrad to robust tensor estimation and derives statistically optimal rates under heavy-tailed noise. We showcase the results of numerical experiments and comparison with prior methods in Section
7
. All the proofs are relegated to the Appendix.

2
Robust Loss and Riemannian Sub-gradient Descent

Without loss of generality, we first focus on the case of matrix estimation. Extension to robust tensor estimation can be found in Section
6
.
Denote
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:=\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}},\textrm{rank}({\mathbf{M}})\leq r\}
the set of matrices with rank bounded by
r
. With a properly selected loss function
\rho(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we aim to solve

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{M}}:=\underset{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}}{\arg\min}\ f({\mathbf{M}}),\quad\textrm{ where }f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\big(\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle-Y_{i}\big)

(2)

The statistical property of
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
crucially relies on the loss function. For instance,
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
attained by the
\ell_{2}
-loss (
square loss
), i.e.,
\rho(x):=x^{2}
, has been proven effective
(
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Wei et al. 2016
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
)
in dealing with sub-Gaussian noise but is well-recognized sensitive to outliers and heavy-tailed noise
(
Huber 1965
)
. Fortunately, there are the so-called
robust
loss functions which are relatively immune to outliers and heavy-tailed noise and capable to deliver a more reliable estimate
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
. Examples of robust loss function include:

1.

absolute loss (
\ell_{1}
-loss):
\rho(x):=|x|
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
;

2.

Huber loss:
\rho_{H,\delta}(x):=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}(|x|\leq\delta)+(2\delta|x|-\delta^{2})\mathbbm{1}(|x|>\delta)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
where
\delta>0
is a tuning parameter.

3.

quantile loss:
\rho_{Q,\delta}(x):=\delta x\mathbbm{1}(x\geq 0)+(\delta-1)x\mathbbm{1}(x<0)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
\delta:={\mathbb{P}}(\xi\leq 0)
.

The absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss are all convex. They have appeared in the literature for noisy low-rank matrix estimation including the convex approach based on nuclear norm penalization
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Klopp et al. 2017
;
Candès et al. 2011
)
and non-convex approach based on gradient-style algorithms
(
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021a
)
.
While our theory developed in Section
3
applies to general (robust) loss functions, the cases of absolute loss
(
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
)
, Huber loss
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Sun et al. 2020
)
and quantile loss
(
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Chen et al.
)
will be specifically investigated in Section
4
. Compared to the square loss, most robust loss functions are non-smooth, i.e., their derivatives are dis-continuous, which brings new challenge to its computation. Indeed, optimizing program (
2
) usually exploits the sub-gradient of robust loss functions, written as
\partial f({\mathbf{M}})
. See, for instance,
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
and references therein.

Due to the low-rank constraint, program (
2
) is non-convex and solvable only locally. One popular tactic to enforce the low-rank constraint is to reparametrize the program (
2
) by factorization
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{U}}{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}
with
r
-columned matrices
{\mathbf{U}}
and
{\mathbf{V}}
. Then it suffices to update
({\mathbf{U}},{\mathbf{V}})
sequentially to minimize (
2
) by local algorithms, e.g., by sub-Gradient descent. These algorithms
(
Zheng and Lafferty 2016
;
Zhao et al. 2015
)
often run fast if
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
is
well-conditioned
, namely
\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\sigma_{r}^{-1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})
is small, where
\sigma_{j}({\mathbf{M}})
denotes the
j
-th singular value of
{\mathbf{M}}
so that
\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{M}})^{\ast}
is the operator norm of
{\mathbf{M}}
. However, they suffer a great loss of computational efficiency if
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
is
ill-conditioned
. Though the issue can be theoretically remedied by a proper inverse scaling
(
Tong et al. 2021a
)
, it can cause a potential computational instability especially when
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
has small non-zero singular values. Recently, it is discovered that optimizing (
2
) directly on the low-rank manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, called
Riemannian
, enjoys the fast computational speed of factorization-based approaches and, meanwhile, converges linearly regardless of the condition number of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
. See, e.g.,
Cai et al. 2021a
and
Cai et al. 2021b
, for the convergence of Riemannian gradient descent (RGrad) algorithms in minimizing
strongly convex and smooth
functions with tensor-related applications. We note that RGrad is similar to the projected gradient descent (PGD,
Chen and Wainwright 2015
) except that RGrad utilizes the Riemannian gradient while PGD takes the vanilla one.

We adapt Riemannian optimization to solve program (
2
). Since
f(\cdot)
can be non-smooth so that the sub-gradient is employed, we refer our algorithm to as the Riemannian sub-gradient (RsGrad) descent. At the
l
-th iteration with a current low-rank estimate
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}
, the algorithm consists of two major steps. It begins with computing the Riemannian sub-gradient, which is the projection of a
vanilla
sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}}_{r})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
onto the tangent space, denoted by
{\mathbb{T}}_{l}
, of
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
at the point
{\mathbf{M}}_{r}
. The benefit of using Riemannian sub-gradient, written as
{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})
, instead of the vanilla one is that the Riemannian sub-gradient is low-rank while the vanilla one is full-rank
(
Cambier and Absil 2016
)
, which significantly boosts up the subsequent singular value decomposition (SVD). Interested readers are suggested to refer to
Vandereycken 2013
;
Wei et al. 2016
;
Cai et al. 2021a
and references therein for more discussions. The second step is to update the low-rank estimate along the direction of negative Riemannian sub-gradient and then retract it back to the manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, for which, it suffices to take the SVD. Here
{\rm SVD}_{r}(\cdot)
returns the best rank-
r
approximation by SVD. The details of RsGrad are presented in Algorithm
1
. Note that the Riemannian sub-gradient algorithm has been introduced by
Cambier and Absil 2016
for minimizing the absolute loss without convergence and statistical analysis. In contrast, our framework covers general robust loss functions, and we prove its computational efficiency and statistical optimality for several important applications.

Algorithm 1

Riemannian Sub-gradient Descent (RsGrad)

Input
: observations
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, max iterations
{l_{\textsf{max}}}
, step sizes
\{\eta_{l}\}_{l=0}^{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}
.

Initialization:
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}

for

l=0,\ldots,{l_{\textsf{max}}}

do

Choose a vanilla subgradient:
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}}_{l})

Compute Riemannian sub-gradient:
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l}={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})

Retraction to
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
:
{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}=\text{SVD}_{r}({\mathbf{M}}_{l}-\eta_{l}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l})

end

for

Output
:
\hat{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{M}}_{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}

Computation.

The Riemannian sub-gradient is fast computable. Let
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}={\mathbf{U}}_{l}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{l}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}^{\top}
be the thin SVD of
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}
. It is well-known
(
Absil et al. 2009
;
Vandereycken 2013
)
that the tangent space
{\mathbb{T}}_{l}
can be characterized by
{\mathbb{T}}_{l}:=\{{\mathbf{Z}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}:{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{U}}_{l}{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}+{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}^{\top},{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}\times r},{\mathbf{L}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times r}\}
. Then, for any
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}
, the projection onto
{\mathbb{T}}_{l}
is

{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})={\mathbf{U}}_{l}{\mathbf{U}}_{l}^{\top}{\mathbf{G}}_{l}+{\mathbf{G}}_{l}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}^{\top}-{\mathbf{U}}_{l}{\mathbf{U}}_{l}^{\top}{\mathbf{G}}_{l}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}{\mathbf{V}}_{l}^{\top},

which is of rank at most
2r
. Consequently, the final step of retraction only requires the SVD of a
2r\times 2r
matrix. See, e.g.,
Vandereycken 2013
and
Mishra et al. 2014
for more details.

3
General Convergence Performance

We now present the general convergence performance of RsGrad Algorithm
1
, which essentially relies on regularity conditions of the objective function. These conditions, in spirit, largely inherit those from existing literature
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Tong et al. 2021a
)
. However, as explained in Section
1
, these prior works only delivered statistically sub-optimal estimates. It turns out that more delicate characterizations of these conditions are necessary for our purpose. More exactly, we discover that the aforementioned robust functions exhibit strikingly different regularity conditions near and far away from
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
, referred to as the
dual-phase
regularity conditions.

Assumption 1
.

(Dual-phase regularity conditions)
Let
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}>0
and define two regions around the truth
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}

{\mathbb{B}}_{1}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\right\}\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathbb{B}}_{2}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\right\}

, where
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
represents the Frobenius norm of a matrix. Suppose the following conditions hold.

1.

(Dual-phase sharpness)
The function
f(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is said to satisfy rank-
r
restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
dual-phase sharpness with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
if

f({\mathbf{M}})-f({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\geq\begin{cases}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2}.\end{cases}

2.

(Dual-phase sub-gradient bound)
The function
f(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has rank-r restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
dual-phase sub-gradient bound with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
meaning that for any sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}})
,

\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,r}\leq\begin{cases}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2}.\end{cases}

Here, the truncated Frobenius norm
\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,r}:=\|{\rm SVD}_{r}({\mathbf{G}})\|_{\rm F}
where
{\rm SVD}_{r}(\cdot)
returns the best rank-
r
approximation of a matrix. See
Tong et al. 2021b
.

Basically, Assumption
1
dictates the distinct behaviors of
f(\cdot)
in two neighbourhoods of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
. The quantity
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
reflects the statistical limit while
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
is usually the rate achieved by the computational analysis in existing literature
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Tong et al. 2021a
)
without assuming noise distributions. These prior works only reveal the first phase regularity conditions, i.e., on
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}
. Interestingly,
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\gg\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
under mild condition on noise distribution, in which case these prior works only deliver statistically sub-optimal estimates. Deriving the second phase regularity condition is challenging, for which more precise calculations are necessary. We remark that, in statistics literature, the sub-gradient bound is related to the Lipschitz continuity
(
Alquier et al. 2019
)
and the sharpness condition is called the
one-point-margin condition

(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
. However, their estimators are built upon convex program so that the analysis is only made in a small neighbour of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
where the dual-phase regularity conditions are not pivotal.

Remark 1
.

The second phase regularity conditions are similar to those required in smooth optimization, e.g., the square loss
(
Cai et al. 2021a
;
Zhao et al. 2015
)
and logistic loss
(
Lyu et al. 2021
)
. For many applications (see Section
4
), the second phase occurs when the individual random noise starts to (stochastically) dominate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
. This suggests that, when close enough to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
, the random noise has the effect of smoothing the objective function. Randomized smoothing has been observed in optimization and statistics literature. See, e.g. ,
Duchi et al. 2012
;
Zhang and Xia 2020
and references therein.

The following proposition establishes the general convergence performance of Algorithm
1
. Note that the results are deterministic under Assumption
1
. Recall that
\sigma_{r}:=\sigma_{r}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})
, the smallest non-zero singular value of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, the initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}\cdot\min\{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\}
for a small but absolute constant
c_{0}>0
, and the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in\left[0.2\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2},\ 0.3\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\right]
. At the
l
-th iteration of Algorithm
1
,

1.

When
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
, namely in phase one, take the stepsize
\eta_{l}=\big(1-0.04\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\big)^{l}\eta_{0}
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\big(1-0.04\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\big)^{l}\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}.

(3)

2.

When
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
, namely in phase two, take stepsize
\eta_{l}\in\big[0.125\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2},\ 0.75\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2}\big]
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}{32L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}\right)\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}.

(4)

By Proposition
1
, the RsGrad Algorithm
1
outputs an estimate
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
within a
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
distance in Frobenius norm from the truth.
As discussed above, phase one is typical non-smooth optimization, for which the stepsize needs to be adaptive. For simplicity, we apply the geometrically decay stepsize
(
Tong et al. 2021a
;
Goffin 1977
)
. Interestingly, phase two is essentially smooth optimization so that a fixed stepsize suffices to yield linear convergence. See, for instance,
Cai et al. 2021a
;
Wei et al. 2016
and references therein. Note that the convergence dynamic is free of the matrix condition number, a benefit of Riemannian-type algorithms. Lastly, we remark that the convergence dynamic is valid only when
\sigma_{r}=\Theta(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
, otherwise the initialization does not belong to either of the two phases.

Comparison with prior works.

The algorithmic dynamic of RsGrad in phase one is similar to that in
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
and
Tong et al. 2021b
, where the geometrically decay of stepsizes in phase one will eventually bring the stepsize into the level
O(\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2})
desired by phase-two convergence. However,
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
and
Tong et al. 2021b
will continue to shrink the stepsize geometrically and fail to deliver a statistically optimal estimator. In contrast, our RsGrad sets a constant stepsize in phase two and the ultimate estimator is statistically optimal. The difference is observed in numerical experiments. See Section
7
for more details.

4
Applications

In this section, specific applications of Proposition
1
are studied and their statistical performances are presented. Since our major interest is on the loss functions and their effectiveness against heavy-tailed noise, for simplicity, we assume the measurement matrix
{\mathbf{X}}
has i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries. While this condition is relaxable to more general sub-Gaussian distributions, they inevitably further complicate the subsequent calculations and is hence not pursued here.

4.1
Absolute loss with Gaussian noise

We begin to demonstrate that the absolute loss, though motivated for heavy-tailed noise, can deliver a statistically optimal estimator even when the noise is Gaussian. Assume
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}
are i.i.d.
N(0,\sigma^{2})
and take the absolute loss so that the objective function

\displaystyle f({\mathbf{M}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle\big|.

(5)

The following lemma affirms the dual-phase regularity properties for the loss function in (
5
).

Lemma 1
.

Assume
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,\sigma^{2})
. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},c_{1}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}rd_{1}
, then absolute loss (
5
) satisfies Assumption
1
with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{1}rd_{1})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\sigma,\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{2}\sigma\Big(\frac{rd_{1}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{n}{12},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{n}{12\sigma}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2n
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{4}n\sigma^{-1}
, respectively. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp\sigma n^{-1}
for Algorithm
1
.

By putting together Lemma
1
and Proposition
1
, we immediately obtain the convergence and statistical performance of RsGrad Algorithm
1
. The proof is a combination of Proposition
1
and Lemma
1
, and is hence omitted.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose the conditions of Lemma
1
hold. There exist absolute constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in(0,1),C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
1
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{1}rd_{1})
, Algorithm
1
has the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\sigma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{2})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\sigma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the statistically sub-optimal rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\sigma
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\sigma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(\log\big(n/(d_{1}r)\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the statistically optimal rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}\sigma(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}
.

Note that, by Proposition
1
, the stepsizes are chosen as
\eta_{l}=(1-0.04\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2})^{l}\eta_{0}
during phase one and set to
\eta_{l}\asymp\sigma n^{-1}
during phase. The initial stepsize
\eta_{0}
can be as large as
O(\sigma_{r}n^{-1})
depending on the initialization.
By Theorem
1
, the final estimate
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
, output by Algorithm
1
with a warm initialization, properly chosen stepsizes and after
\Theta\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\sigma)+\log(n/(rd_{1}))\big)
iterations, achieves the rate
\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O_{p}(\sigma^{2}rd_{1}n^{-1})
that is minimax optimal. See
Ma and Wu 2015
;
Xia 2014
for a matching minimax lower bound. In comparison, prior works
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2019
;
Tong et al. 2021b
)
only achieve the rate
O_{p}(\sigma^{2})
. The two phase dynamics are observed in numerical experiments. See Section
7
for more details.

4.2
Absolute loss with heavy-tailed noise

In this section, we demonstrate the effectiveness of absolute loss in handling heavy-tailed noise. More specifically, the following assumption of noise is required. Denote
h_{\xi}(\cdot)
and
H_{\xi}(\cdot)
the density and distribution function of the noise, respectively.

Assumption 2
.

(Heavy-tailed noise I)
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. The noise has median zero, i.e.,
H_{\xi}(0)=1/2
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}>0
(may be dependent on
\gamma
) such that

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\geq b_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 30\gamma;

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\leq b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

By Assumption
2
, a simple fact is
b_{0}\geq 30\gamma
. The noise is required to have a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment, which is to bound
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\xi_{i}|
. This is fairly weak compared with existing literature. For instance,
Minsker 2018
requires a finite second-order moment condition on noise;
Fan et al. 2021
imposes a
2+\varepsilon
moment condition. The lower bound on density function is similar to that required by
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
. The upper bound condition on density function is also mild. For example, a Lipschitz distribution function ensures such a uniform upper bound.

The dual-phase regularity condition of the absolute loss with heavy-tailed noise is guaranteed as follows. Notice if a constant factor depends on
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
or/and
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\epsilon}
, a star sign is placed on top left of it.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose Assumption
2
holds. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}rd_{1}
, then the absolute loss (
5
) satisfies Assumption
1
with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=30\gamma,\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{2}b_{0}\Big(\frac{rd_{1}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{n}{6},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{n}{12b_{0}}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2n
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{3}nb_{1}^{-1}
, respectively. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
for Algorithm
1
.

Viewing
b_{0}/\gamma
as a constant, then
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\ll\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
if the sample size is large. Equipped with Lemma
2
, the convergence and statistical performance of RsGrad Algorithm
1
under heavy-tailed noise is guaranteed by the following theorem. Note that if a constant factor depends on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
, a star sign is placed on top right of it.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose Assumption
2
and the conditions of Lemma
2
hold. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}^{\ast}\in(0,1),C_{1}^{\ast},C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
1
, then with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
, Algorithm
1
has the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq 30\gamma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq 30\gamma
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq 30\gamma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{4}^{\ast})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(C_{1}^{\ast}\log\big(\gamma n/(d_{1}r)\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}b_{0}(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}
.

By Theorem
2
, if
b_{0}/\gamma
is a constant, Algorithm
1
outputs a final estimate achieving the rate
\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}=O_{p}\big({\mathbb{E}}|\xi|\cdot(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}\big)
. Note that Gaussian noise also satisfy Assumption
2
, in which case the aforesaid rate has a matching minimax lower bound. Therefore, we claim this rate to be minimax optimal. Moreover, our estimator computes fast and requires only a logarithmic factor of iterations.

4.3
Huber loss with heavy-tailed noise

Huber loss is prevalent in robust statistics
(
Huber 1965
;
Sun et al. 2020
;
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
and defined by
\rho_{H,\delta}(x):=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}(|x|\leq\delta)+(2\delta|x|-\delta^{2})\mathbbm{1}(|x|>\delta)
where
\delta>0
is referred to as the robustification parameter. Clearly, the function
\rho_{H,\delta}
is Lipschitz with a constant
2\delta
. Then the loss function is given by

\displaystyle f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{H,\delta}(Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle)

(6)

Huber loss is robust to heavy-tailed noise but it turns out that a slightly different assumption is needed for our purpose.

Assumption 3
.

(Heavy-tailed noise II)
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. The noise has a symmetric distribution, i.e.,
H_{\xi}(x)=1-H_{\xi}(-x)
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}
(may be dependent on
\gamma
and
\delta
) such that

\displaystyle H_{\xi}(x+\delta)-H_{\xi}(x-\delta)\geq 2\delta b_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 24\gamma+2\delta;

\displaystyle H_{\xi}(x+\delta)-H_{\xi}(x-\delta)\leq 2\delta b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}

, where
\delta
is the Huber loss parameter.

Compared with Assumption
2
, here the noise is required to be symmetric but the condition on density function is relaxed. The dual-phase regularity condition of Huber loss is ensured by the following lemma. If a constant factor depends on
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
or/and
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\epsilon}
, a star sign is placed on top left of it.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose Assumption
3
holds. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}rd_{1}
, then the Huber loss (
6
) satisfies Assumption
1
with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=24\gamma+2\delta,\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{\delta,1}b_{0}\Big(\frac{rd_{1}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{\delta n}{4},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{\delta n}{3b_{0}}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2\delta n
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{\delta,2}nb_{1}^{-1}
, respectively, where
C_{\delta,1}=C_{3}\cdot\max\{1,\delta^{-1}\}
and
C_{\delta,2}=C_{3}\cdot\max\{1,\delta\}
. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
for Algorithm
1
.

Proposition
1
and Lemma
3
lead to the following convergence and statistical performance of RsGrad Algorithm
1
for Huber loss. Similarly, the constants dependent on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
are marked with star.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose Assumption
3
and the conditions of Lemma
3
hold. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}^{\ast}\in(0,1),C_{1,\delta}^{\ast},C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
1
, then with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
, Algorithm
1
has the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq 24\gamma+2\delta
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq 24\gamma+2\delta
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq 24\gamma+2\delta
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{4}^{\ast})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(C_{1}^{\ast}\log\big(\gamma n/(d_{1}r)\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}\max\{1,\delta^{-1}\}\cdot b_{0}(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}
.

By Theorem
3
, RsGrad algorithm outputs a final estimate
\hat{\mathbf{M}}
with Frobenius-norm error
O\big(\max\{1,\delta^{-1}\}\cdot b_{0}(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}\big)
. Assumption
3
implies that
b_{0}
is at least lower bounded by
2(\gamma+\delta)
and thus the error rate is lower bounded by
\max\{\delta+\gamma,1+\gamma\delta^{-1}\}(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}\big)
. This suggests an interesting transition with respect to the robustification parameter: when
\delta\leq\gamma
, increasing
\delta
does not affect the error rate; when
\delta\geq\max\{\gamma,1\}
, the error rate appreciates if
\delta
becomes larger. Therefore, an appropriate choice can be
\delta\asymp\gamma
. In contrast, by Theorem
2
, the absolute loss achieves an ultimate estimator with a comparable error rate but is free of the tuning parameter
\delta
. Note that, though absolute loss is a special case of Huber loss (i.e.,
\delta=0
), our Theorem
3
is not directly applicable to the absolute loss due to proof technicality.
Finally,
Sun et al. 2020
derived the statistical performance of Huber loss for linear regression without imposing regularity conditions on noise density, resulting into
an error rate that is possibly slower than the traditional
O(n^{-1/2})
rate.

4.4
Quantile loss with heavy-tailed noise

Quantile loss was initially proposed by
Koenker and Bassett Jr 1978
and has been a popular loss function for robust statistics
(
Welsh 1989
;
Koenker and Hallock 2001
;
Wang et al. 2012
)
. Denote the quantile loss by
\rho_{Q,\delta}:=\delta x\mathbbm{1}(x\geq 0)+(\delta-1)x\mathbbm{1}(x<0)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
\delta:={\mathbb{P}}(\xi\leq 0)
. Notice that the function
\rho_{Q,\delta}
is Lipschitz with a constant
\max\{\delta,1-\delta\}
. The loss function is given by

\displaystyle f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{Q,\delta}(Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle).

(7)

Absolute loss could be viewed as a special case of quantile loss when
{\mathbb{P}}(\xi\leq 0)=1/2
, i.e., noise has median zero. Similarly, a slightly different assumption on the heavy-tailed noise is necessary for quantile loss.

Assumption 4
.

(Heavy-tailed noise III)
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. Suppose
\delta:=H_{\xi}(0)
lies in
(0,1)
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}>0
(may be dependent on
\gamma
) such that

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\geq b_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 15\gamma\max\{\delta,1-\delta\};

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\leq b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

When the noise has median zero, namely
\delta=1/2
, Assumption
4
becomes identical to Assumption
2
. The dual-phase regularity of the quantile loss is provided by the following lemma. Notice if a constant factor depends on
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
or/and
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\epsilon}
, a star sign is placed on top left of it.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose Assumption
4
holds. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}rd_{1}
, then the absolute loss (
7
) satisfies Assumption
1
with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=15\gamma\max\{\frac{1}{\delta},\frac{1}{1-\delta}\},\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{2}b_{0}\Big(\frac{rd_{1}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{n}{6}\min\{\delta,1-\delta\},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{n}{12b_{0}}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2n\max\{\delta,1-\delta\}
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{3}nb_{1}^{-1}
, respectively. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
for Algorithm
1
.

By combining Proposition
1
with Lemma
4
, we get the convergence dynamics and statistical accuracy of RsGrad Algorithm
1
for quantile loss. Same as previous, those constants dependent on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
are marked with a star at top.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose Assumption
4
and the conditions of Lemma
4
hold. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}^{\ast}\in(0,1),C_{1}^{\ast},C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
1
, then with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})
, Algorithm
1
has the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq 15\gamma\max\{\delta^{-1},(1-\delta)^{-1}\}
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3}\min\{\delta(1-\delta)^{-1},(1-\delta)\delta^{-1}\})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\min\{\delta,1-\delta\}\cdot\sigma_{r}/\gamma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq 15\gamma\max\{\delta^{-1},(1-\delta)^{-1}\}
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq 15\gamma\max\{\delta^{-1},(1-\delta)^{-1}\}
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{4}^{\ast})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(C_{1}^{\ast}\log\big(\max\{\delta^{-1},(1-\delta)^{-1}\}\cdot\gamma n/(d_{1}r)\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}b_{0}(rd_{1}/n)^{1/2}
.

Parameter
\delta
is kept in all results to show the explicit dependence on
\delta
. Theorem
4
shows the final estimator
\hat{{\mathbf{M}}}
output by RsGrad Algorithm
1
for quantile loss achieves Frobenius-norm error
O(C_{2}b_{0}(d_{1}r/n)^{1/2})
.

5
Initialization and Algorithmic Parameters Selection

Initialization.

Convergence of Algorithm
1
crucially relies on the warm initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
. Towards that end, one can simply apply the shrinkage low-rank approximation as in
Fan et al. 2021
where a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment condition on noise is required. Here, for simplicity, we investigate the performance of vanilla low-rank approximation.

Theorem 5
.

(Initialization Guarantees)
Suppose there exists some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
such that
\gamma_{1}:={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. Initialize
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}:=\operatorname{SVD}_{r}(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}Y_{i})
.
For any small
c_{0}>0
, there exist constants
C,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
c_{0}
such that if the sample size

n>C\max\big\{\kappa^{2}d_{1}r^{2}\log d_{1},(d_{1}r)^{\frac{1+\epsilon}{2\epsilon}}\sigma_{r}^{-\frac{1+\epsilon}{\epsilon}}(\gamma_{1}\log d_{1})^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\big\},

with probability over
1-c_{1}d_{1}^{-1}-c_{2}\log^{-1}d_{1}-c_{3}e^{-d_{1}}
, the initialization satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}
, where
\kappa:=\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\sigma_{r}^{-1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})
denotes the condition number.

Compared with
Fan et al. 2021
, Theorem
5
only requires a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment condition and a similar sample size condition. However, the vanilla low-rank approximation demands a much larger signal-to-noise ratio condition. Since the shrinkage-based approaches
(
Fan et al. 2021
;
Minsker 2018
)
are already effective to provide warm initializations, we spare no additional efforts to improve Theorem
5
.

Selection of initial stepsize.

According to Proposition
1
, the initial stepsize depends on
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
,
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
. Fortunately, as shown in Lemma
1
-
4
for specific applications, the quantities
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
are often of order
O(n)
up to a factor of loss-related parameters. It suffices to obtain an estimate of
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
. Towards that end, one appropriate method is to take the average
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}}_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|
, which, under mild conditions, is of the same order of
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
with high probability. Besides, a simpler way is to directly use the operator norm
c\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\|
to replace
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
where
c>0
is a tuning parameter, if we believe that
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
is indeed of order
O(\sigma_{r})
.

Determine the phase.

Due to the geometrically decay of stepsizes during phase one, after some iterations, the stepsize will enter the level
O(\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2})
desired by phase two convergence. On the other hand, if phase one iterations continue, the stepsize will diminish fast and the value of objective function becomes stable. This is indeed observed in numerical experiments. See Figure
1
and Figure
2
in Section
7
. Therefore, once the stepsize falls below a pre-chosen small threshold, the phase two iterations can be initiated and the stepsizes are fixed afterwards until convergence.

6
Extension to Robust Low-rank Tensor Estimation

We now extend RsGrad to low-rank tensor estimation equipped with a robust loss function and investigate its performance under Gaussian or heavy-tailed noise. Without loss of generality, only the absolute loss is considered here.

Preliminaries in Tucker tensors.

An
m
-th order tensor is an
m
-dimensional array.
For an
m
-th order tensor
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
, denote its mode-
j
matricization as
{\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{j}\times d_{j}^{-}}
, where
d_{j}^{-}:=\prod_{l\neq j}d_{l}
.
The mode-
j
marginal multiplication between
{\mathbf{M}}
and a matrix
{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{j}\times d_{j}}
results into an
m
-th order tensor of size
d_{1}\times\cdots d_{j-1}\times r_{j}\times d_{j+1}\cdots d_{m}
, whose elements are
({\mathbf{M}}\times_{j}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})_{i_{1}\cdots i_{j-1}li_{j+1}\cdots i_{m}}:=\sum_{i_{j}=1}^{d_{j}}{\mathbf{M}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{j-1}i_{j}i_{j+1}\cdots i_{m}}{\mathbf{U}}_{i_{j}l}.
A simple fact is
{\mathbf{N}}_{(j)}={\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}
.
There exist multiple definitions of tensor ranks. We focus on Tucker ranks and the associated Tucker decomposition
(
Tucker 1966
)
.
{\mathbf{M}}
is said to have Tucker rank
{\mathbf{r}}:=(r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{m})
if its mode-
j
matricization has rank
r_{j}
, i.e.,
r_{j}=\text{rank}({\mathbf{M}}_{(j)})
.
Then
{\mathbf{M}}
admits the Tucker decomposition
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{C}}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}\rrbracket:={\mathbf{C}}\times_{1}{\mathbf{U}}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{m}{\mathbf{U}}_{m}
where the core tensor
{\mathbf{C}}
is of size
r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{m}
and
{\mathbf{U}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{j}\times r_{j}}
has orthonormal columns.
Denote
{\mathbb{M}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
be set of all tensors with Tucker rank at most
{\mathbf{r}}
, namely,

{\mathbb{M}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}=\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\dots\times d_{m}}:\text{rank}({\mathbf{M}}_{(j)})\leq r_{j},\,\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,m\}.
Interested readers are suggested to refer to
Kolda and Bader 2009
;
De Silva and Lim 2008
;
De Lathauwer et al. 2000
for more details about Tucker ranks and Tucker decomposition.

Let
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. observations satisfying the trace regression model (
1
) with
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
that, for simplicity, is assumed to have i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries. Suppose the underlying tensor
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
. Our goal is to reliably estimate
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
under possibly heavy-tailed noise and using as few observations as possible.
Towards that end, we aim to solve

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathbf{M}}}:=\underset{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathbf{r}}}{\arg\min}\ f({\mathbf{M}}),\quad\textrm{ where }f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\big(\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle-Y_{i}\big)

(8)

with
\rho(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
a proper chosen robust loss function. RsGrad is readily applicable to solve program (
8
) except that now it operates on the tensor Riemannian and its sub-gradient needs to be taken carefully.

The tensor-version RsGrad is presented in Algorithm
2
. Note that the Riemannian sub-gradient is explicitly computed as follows. Let
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}={\mathbf{C}}_{l}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{(l)},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{(l)}\rrbracket
be its Tucker decomposition. Given the vanilla sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}
, we have

{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})={\mathbf{G}}_{l}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{(l)}{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{(l)\top},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{(l)}{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{(l)\top}\rrbracket+\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbf{C}}_{l}\times_{j\in[m]\backslash i}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{(l)}\times_{i}\dot{{\mathbf{U}}}_{i}^{(l)}

where
\dot{{\mathbf{U}}}_{i}^{(l)}
is defined by
({\mathbf{I}}-{\mathbf{U}}_{i}^{(l)}{\mathbf{U}}_{i}^{(l)\top})({\mathbf{G}}_{l})_{(i)}(\otimes_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{l})({\mathbf{C}}_{l})_{(i)}^{\dagger}
. The Tucker rank of
{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})
is at most
2{\mathbf{r}}
. The retraction step in Algorithm
2
relies on high-order SVD (HOSVD,
De Lathauwer et al. 2000
,
Xia and Zhou 2019
), which is obtained by
\text{HOSVD}_{{\mathbf{r}}}({\mathbf{M}}):={\mathbf{M}}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1}{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{\top},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{\top}\rrbracket
with
{\mathbf{U}}_{j}
being the top-
r_{j}
left singular vectors of
{\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}
for all
j\in[m]
.

Algorithm 2

Riemannian Sub-gradient Descent for Tensor (RsGrad)

Input
: observations
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, max iterations
{l_{\textsf{max}}}
, step sizes
\{\eta_{l}\}_{l=0}^{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}
.

Initialization:
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathbf{r}}

for

l=0,\ldots,{l_{\textsf{max}}}

do

Choose a vanilla subgradient:
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}}_{l})

Compute Riemannian sub-gradient:
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l}={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})

Retraction to
{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathbf{r}}
:
{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}=\text{HOSVD}_{\mathbf{r}}({\mathbf{M}}_{l}-\eta_{l}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l})

end

for

Output
:
\hat{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{M}}_{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}

6.1
General Convergence Performance

Similarly, we establish the general convergence performance of Algorithm
2
under the dual-phase regularity conditions. The following assumption is almost identical to Assumption
1
except that the matrix SVD is replaced by tensor low-rank approximation.

Assumption 5
.

(Dual-phase regularity conditions for tensor)
Let
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}>0
and define two regions around the truth
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}

{\mathbb{B}}_{1}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathbf{r}}:\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\right\}\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathbb{B}}_{2}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathbf{r}}:\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\right\}

, where
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
represents the Frobenius norm of a tensor. Suppose the following conditions hold.

1.

(Dual-phase sharpness)
The function
f(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is said to satisfy rank-
{\mathbf{r}}
restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
dual-phase sharpness with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
if

f({\mathbf{M}})-f({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\geq\begin{cases}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2}.\end{cases}

2.

(Dual-phase sub-gradient bound)
The function
f(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has rank-r restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
dual-phase sub-gradient bound with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
meaning that for any sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}})
,

\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,2{\mathbf{r}}}\leq\begin{cases}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2}.\end{cases}

The truncated Frobenius norm
\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,2{\mathbf{r}}}:=\sup_{{\mathbf{U}}_{j}}\|{\mathbf{G}}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{\top},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{\top}\rrbracket\|_{\rm F}
where
{\mathbf{U}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times 2r_{j}}
has orthonormal columns.

Under Assumption
5
and with a warm initialization, the following Proposition
2
shows that RsGrad converges linearly and attains the statistical error rate
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
. The signal strength of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
is defined by
\underline{\lambda}:=\min_{j=1,\cdots,m}\{\sigma_{r_{j}}({\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}^{*})\}
that is the smallest nonzero singular value among all matricizations of
{\mathbf{M}}^{*}
.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose Assumption
5
holds, the initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
satisfies

\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\left\{\frac{1}{m+1}\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}},\ \frac{1}{m+1}\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}},\ \frac{1}{m(m+3)}\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}},\ \frac{1}{m(m+3)}\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}}\right\}

for a small but absolute constant
c_{0}>0
, and the initial stepsize

\eta_{0}\in\left[0.25(m+1)^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2},\ 0.75(m+1)^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\right].

At the
l
-th iteration of Algorithm
2
,

1.

When
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
, namely in phase one, take the stepsize
\eta_{l}=\big(1-\frac{1}{16(m+1)}\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}\big)^{l}\eta_{0}
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{16(m+1)}\cdot\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}\right)^{l}\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}.

(9)

2.

When
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
, namely in phase two, take stepsize
\eta_{l}\in\big[0.25(m+1)^{-1}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2},\ 0.75(m+1)^{-1}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2}\big]
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{16(m+1)}\cdot\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}\right)\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}.

(10)

Note that
m
is kept explicitly in Proposition
2
to underscore its role in the convergence of RsGrad algorithm. The convergence dynamic of RsGrad during phase one is similar to that of ScaledSM
(
Tong et al. 2022
)
, both of which achieve the computational accuracy
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}})
. However, under the dual-phase regularity condition, RsGrad presents a two-phase dynamic which converges linearly with a constant step size and eventually achieves a statistical error rate
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
, which is usually significantly smaller than
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}})
.

6.2
Applications

We now specifically investigate the statistical performances of RsGrad for absolute loss under Gaussian noise and heavy-tailed noise. The results of Huber loss and quantile loss can be similarly established and skipped here.

6.2.1
Absolute loss with Gaussian noise

Suppose that the noise
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}
are i.i.d.
N(0,\sigma^{2})
. Take the absolute loss and get the objective function
f({\mathbf{M}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|.
Denote
\textsf{DoF}_{m}:=2^{m}\cdot r_{1}r_{2}\cdots r_{m}+2\sum_{j=1}^{m}d_{j}r_{j}
, reflecting the model complexity. If
m
is a constant, then
\textsf{DoF}_{m}
can be treated as the degree of freedom. The dual-phase regularity condition is guaranteed by Lemma
5
.

Lemma 5
.

Assume
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,\sigma^{2})
. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},c_{1}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m}
, then absolute loss satisfies Assumption
5
with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{1}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\sigma,\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{2}\sigma\Big(\frac{\textsf{DoF}_{m}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{n}{12},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{n}{12\sigma}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2n
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{4}n\sigma^{-1}
, respectively. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp\sigma n^{-1}
for Algorithm
2
.

The following theorem presents the convergence dynamic and statistical accuracy of Algorithm
2
for absolute loss under Gaussian noise.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose the conditions of Lemma
5
hold. There exist absolute constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in(0,1),C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}m^{-1}(m+3)^{-1}\underline{\lambda}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
2
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{1}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m})
, Algorithm
2
exhibits the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\sigma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{2})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\underline{\lambda}/\sigma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the statistically sub-optimal rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\sigma
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\sigma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(\log\big(n/\textsf{DoF}_{m}\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the statistically optimal rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}\sigma(\textsf{DoF}_{m}\cdot n^{-1})^{1/2}
.

By Theorem
6
, Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator achieving statistical rate
\|\hat{{\mathbf{M}}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}=O_{p}(\sigma^{2}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m}\cdot n^{-1})
. The rate matches the existing ones attained by the square loss
(
Cai et al. 2021a
;
Han et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2020
)
. Theorem
6
only requires a sample complexity
O(\textsf{DoF}_{m})
to ensure the algorithmic convergence. Oftentimes, the sample size requirement is more stringent to guarantee a warm initialization. See Section
6.3
.

6.2.2
Absolute loss with heavy-tailed noise

Now consider the performance of absolute loss under heavy-tailed noise.
The following lemma certifies the dual-phase regularity under a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment condition on noise. Hereafter, a star sign on the top left of a constant indicates that it depends on the
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
or/and
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose Assumption
2
holds. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m}
, then the absolute loss satisfies Assumption
5
with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m})
where

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=30\gamma,\ \tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=C_{2}b_{0}\Big(\frac{\textsf{DoF}_{m}}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}=\frac{n}{6},\ {\rm and}\ \mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}=\frac{n}{12b_{0}}

, moreover, the dual-phase sub-gradient bounds are
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\leq 2n
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\leq C_{3}nb_{1}^{-1}
, respectively. This implies the second phase step size
\eta\asymp b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
for Algorithm
2
.

Proposition
2
and Lemma
6
immediately imply the convergence dynamic and statistical error as presented in the following theorem. Similarly, constant factors with a star sign on its top right corner depends on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose Assumption
2
and the conditions of Lemma
6
hold. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},{}^{*}\!c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}^{\ast}\in(0,1),C_{1}^{\ast},C_{2}>0
such that if the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}m^{-1}(m+3)^{-1}\underline{\lambda}
, the stepsizes are chosen as in Proposition
2
, then with probability at least
1-{}^{*}\!c_{1}n^{-\varepsilon}-\exp(-c_{2}\cdot\textsf{DoF}_{m})
, Algorithm
2
has the following dynamics:

1.

during phase one when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l-1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq 30\gamma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{3})^{l}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{1}=\Theta\big(\log(\underline{\lambda}/\gamma)\big)
iterations in phase one, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq 30\gamma
;

2.

during phase two when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq 30\gamma
, the updated estimate satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq(1-c_{4}^{\ast})\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}+l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
; after
l_{2}=\Theta\big(C_{1}^{\ast}\log\big(\gamma n/\textsf{DoF}_{m}\big)\big)
iterations in phase two, it achieves the rate
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{2}+l_{1}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{2}b_{0}(\textsf{DoF}_{m}\cdot n^{-1})^{1/2}
.

By Theorem
7
, as long as the noise has a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment, RsGrad outputs an estimator achieving the statistical rate
\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O_{p}(b_{0}^{2}\textsf{DoF}_{m}\cdot n^{-1})
. If
m
is a constant, this rate is proportional to the degree of freedom and optimal in terms of tensor dimensions. To our best knowledge, this is the first result of this kind for robust low-rank tensor regression under heavy-tailed noise.

6.3
Initialization by Shrinkage-based Second Order Moment

The convergence Theorems
6
and
7
relies crucially on the warm initialization. With heavy-tailed noise, a simple spectral initialization, say, by HOSVD, has limited performances. Adapting from the ideas in
Cai et al. 2021b
;
Xia and Yuan 2017
;
Xia et al. 2021
, we propose a new initialization by shrinkage-based second order moment method. For each response
Y_{i}
, define its shrinkage by
\tilde{Y}_{i}:=\text{sign}(Y_{i})(|Y_{i}|\vee\tau)
with a threshold
\tau>0
to be chosen later.

With the truncated responses, construct the second-order U-statistic :

\tilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq i^{\prime}\leq n}\tilde{Y}_{i}\tilde{Y}_{i^{\prime}}({\mathbf{X}}_{i(j)}{\mathbf{X}}_{i^{\prime}(j)}^{\top}+{\mathbf{X}}_{i^{\prime}(j)}{\mathbf{X}}_{i(j)}^{\top}),\quad j=1,\ldots,m

We will prove that
\tilde{{\mathbf{N}}}_{j}
is a good estimate of
{\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}_{(j)}^{*\top}
with a properly chosen
\tau
and a reasonably large sample size. The top-
r_{j}
left singular vectors of
\tilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}
, denoted by
{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{(0)}
, serve as a warm initial estimate of the mode-
j
singular vectors of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
. Then the initial core tensor is obtained by minimizing the sum of squares that admits an explicit form by

{\mathbf{C}}^{(0)}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}\times_{j\in[m]}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{(0)\top})\cdot\text{vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}\times_{j\in[m]}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{(0)\top})^{\top}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\text{vec}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\times_{j\in[m]}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{(0)\top}\right).

Finally, the initialization for Algorithm
2
is constructed by
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}={\mathbf{C}}^{(0)}\cdot\llbracket{\mathbf{U}}_{1}^{(0)},\cdots,{\mathbf{U}}_{m}^{(0)}\rrbracket
.

By assuming a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment condition, the following theorem confirms the closeness between
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
and
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
.
For ease of exposition, denote
\bar{d}=\max_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}
,
\bar{r}=\max_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}
,
d^{*}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
and
r^{\ast}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
. Recall that
\textsf{DoF}_{m}=2^{m}r^{\ast}+2\sum_{j}r_{j}d_{j}
and the signal strength
\underline{\lambda}
. Denote
\bar{\lambda}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{(j)}\|
and the tensor condition number
\kappa:=\bar{\lambda}\underline{\lambda}^{-1}
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
c_{0}\in(0,1)
be a constant.
Suppose
\|\xi\|_{2+\varepsilon}:=\left({\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{2+\varepsilon}\right)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}<+\infty
for some
\varepsilon>0
and the following conditions hold:

(1)

sample size requirement:
n\geq C_{1}m^{2}\kappa^{6}\bar{r}^{2}(d^{\ast})^{1/2}\log\bar{d}
;

(2)

signal to noise ratio:
\underline{\lambda}\geq C_{2}m^{1/2}\kappa^{1/2}(\bar{r}\log\bar{d})^{1/4}\frac{(d^{\ast})^{1/4}}{n^{1/2}}\|\xi\|_{2+\varepsilon}
,

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are some constants depending only on
c_{0}
. There exist absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
c_{0}
, such that, if
\tau=n^{1/2}{(d^{*})^{-1/4}}(\sqrt{\bar{r}}\bar{\lambda}+\|\xi\|_{2+\varepsilon})
, then with probability exceeding
1-c_{1}\exp(-\textsf{DoF}_{m})-c_{2}n^{-\min\{\varepsilon/2,1\}}-c_{3}m\bar{d}^{-10}
, the initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
satisfies

\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\underline{\lambda}.

If
m,\bar{r}
and
\kappa
are all
O(1)
, Theorem
8
requires a sample size
(d^{\ast})^{1/2}\log\bar{d}
, which matches the best known result in existing literature
(
Han et al. 2022
;
Tong et al. 2022
;
Xia et al. 2021
)
, be it noiseless or under Gaussian noise. The SNR condition becomes
\underline{\lambda}/\|\xi\|_{2+\xi}\gtrsim n^{-1/2}(d^{\ast}\log\bar{d})^{1/4}
that reduces to the typical SNR condition under Gaussian noise in the aforementioned prior works. These conditions are thus likely minimal but here only a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment is necessary.

7
Numerical Experiments

7.1
Low-rank Matrix Estimation

In this section, numerical simulation results are presented which showcase the dual-phase convergence of Algorithm
1
and demonstrate its merit over competing methods
(
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Candes and Plan 2011
)
. Both RsGrad and ScaledSM
(
Tong et al. 2021b
)
require a careful design of stepsize schedule.
Practically, for ScaledSM and phase one of RsGrad, we initialize and update the stepsizes by
\eta_{0}=c_{1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\|n^{-1}
and
\eta_{l}=q\cdot\eta_{l-1},
where
c_{1}>0
,
0<q<1
are parameters to be specified and
\|\cdot\|
denotes the matrix operator norm. As
Tong et al. 2021b
suggests, a great choice is
q=0.91
. For a fair comparison, all the following simulations are implemented with
q=0.91
. As suggested by Proposition
1
, RsGrad has a second phase convergence, for which we set the stepsize at
\eta_{l}=c_{2}{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|n^{-1}
where
c_{2}>0
is the parameter to be tuned. Note that we assume
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
is known for simplicity. In practice,
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
is unknown and can be estimated by
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|
where
{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}
is the output after phase one iterations.
Following
Tong et al. 2021b
, we define signal-to-noise ratio by
\text{SNR}:=20\log_{10}\big(\|{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}/{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|\big)
.

Convergence dynamics

We compare the convergence dynamics between RsGrad and ScaledSM
(
Tong et al. 2021b
)
. Without loss of generality, we only experiment RsGrad with absolute loss (RsGrad-
\ell_{1}
) and Huber loss (RsGrad-Huber). ScaledSM is proposed only for absolute loss. The relative error is calculated by
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\|{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{-1}
.

We fix dimension
d_{1}=d_{2}=80
and rank
r=5
. The first set of simulations is to showcase the convergence performance of RsGrad and ScaledSM under Gaussian noise. The SNR is set at
\{40,80\}
and the sample sizes are varied among
\{2.5,5\}\times rd_{1}
. The convergence dynamics are displayed in Figure
1
, which clearly show a dual-phase convergence of RsGrad. We note that ScaledSM performs poorly when sample size is small (
n=2.5rd_{1}
). It is possibly due to the instability of inverse scaling under small sample size. The bottom two plots in Figure
1
show that, after the phase one iterations, RsGrad achieves a similar perfomrance as ScaledSM. However, with a second phase convergence, RsGrad (using either Huber or absolute loss) eventually delivers a more accurate estimate than ScaledSM.

(a)

SNR=40

(b)

SNR=80

(c)

SNR=40

(d)

SNR=80

Figure 1
:
Convergence dynamics of RsGrad and ScaledSM
(
